> 1. The shift from the multi-store model to the working memory model was driven partly by neuropsychological case studies of brain-damaged patients. What does this illustrate about the relationship between clinical evidence and theoretical development in cognitive science?
> 2. Working memory capacity correlates with reading comprehension, reasoning, and academic achievement. Does this suggest that working memory capacity is a fundamental cognitive resource, or might the correlation reflect shared variance with other constructs like intelligence or processing speed?
> 3. If working memory is the site of real-time cognitive work, what are the implications for how learners should structure their study environments and pacing strategies?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. Baddeley and Hitch's original data showed that occupying the STS with verbal material impaired reasoning somewhat, but not catastrophically. What does this finding imply about the relationship between component-specific resources and general attentional resources in the working memory model?
> 2. The dual-task methodology infers component structure from patterns of interference. What are the epistemological limitations of inferring internal cognitive architecture from behavioral interference patterns? What alternative evidence could converge on the same conclusions?
> 3. Baddeley himself called the central executive a "ragbag" component. What does this acknowledgment of theoretical incompleteness suggest about the scientific value of models that deliberately leave portions underdeveloped?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. The word-length effect shows that digit span varies across cultures because languages differ in how long it takes to pronounce their numerals. What does this imply about the relationship between cognitive architecture (universal) and cognitive performance (culturally variable)?
> 2. The irrelevant speech effect demonstrates that the phonological store captures incoming speech involuntarily, even when we intend to ignore it. What are the implications of this automatic capture for the design of learning environments and the limits of voluntary attentional control?
> 3. The phonological loop is implicated in foreign language acquisition as well as comprehension. Does this suggest that working memory serves both a temporary processing function and a long-term learning function? How might these two functions be related?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. Neuroimaging suggests that visual working memory recruits perceptual cortex rather than maintaining information in a separate "buffer." Does this finding support or challenge the idea that the visuospatial sketchpad is a distinct component from attention and perception?
> 2. If visual working memory involves controlled re-activation of perceptual systems, what are the implications for learning from diagrams, animations, and physical manipulatives versus verbal description alone?
> 3. Individual differences in visuospatial working memory predict performance in STEM fields. What ethical and pedagogical implications follow from this finding, particularly given evidence that this capacity can be trained?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. Engle and Kane's research suggests that working memory capacity differences are primarily differences in controlled attention, not storage capacity. What methodological approaches could distinguish these two possibilities, and what evidence currently favors the controlled attention account?
> 2. The central executive interfaces with long-term memory to activate relevant schemas. How does this interaction explain why experts can handle more complex information in a domain than novices — is it that their central executive is stronger, or that long-term memory reduces the demands on the executive?
> 3. If the central executive is the seat of voluntary cognitive control, what practices (metacognitive, behavioral, environmental) are most effective at amplifying its effective capacity or reducing unnecessary demands on it?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. The episodic buffer is proposed to be accessed through conscious awareness. What are the implications of this claim for the relationship between attention, consciousness, and working memory? Does consciousness play an active role in working memory operations, or is it a byproduct?
> 2. If the episodic buffer is the site where complex multimodal representations are temporarily assembled, what instructional strategies would most effectively engage the buffer during learning, and how might this engagement differ from strategies that primarily recruit the phonological loop?
> 3. Baddeley added the episodic buffer partly in response to empirical anomalies in his original model. What does this theoretical development suggest about the scientific virtues of a framework that can be modified to accommodate evidence, versus one that remains theoretically stable?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. Cowan's model locates working memory in activated long-term memory rather than dedicated short-term stores. What evidence would most strongly distinguish this account from Baddeley's modular model, and what practical implications would follow from adopting Cowan's architecture?
> 2. Working memory training produces robust near-transfer but not far-transfer. What theoretical account best explains this pattern — is it a training artifact, a specificity of learning, or something fundamental about the cognitive architecture?
> 3. Individual differences in working memory capacity are among the strongest cognitive predictors of academic outcomes. Does this mean working memory capacity is the key bottleneck in learning? What factors could explain the correlation without it being the causal bottleneck?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. The expertise reversal effect implies that optimal instruction is learner-specific — what works for novices often fails for experts. What are the implications of this for educational systems that must deliver instruction at scale? What technological or pedagogical approaches best address this challenge?
> 2. Cognitive Load Theory was developed explicitly from Baddeley's working memory model. How would the predictions of CLT change if Cowan's embedded-processes model were adopted as its cognitive foundation instead? Would any principles would survive unchanged, and which would require revision?
> 3. [[Metacognitive-Self-Regulation]] involves learners monitoring their own cognitive load and adjusting strategies accordingly. What are the implications of CLT for metacognitive strategy instruction — particularly for self-regulated learners who can detect their own extraneous load?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. ADHD and dyslexia show distinct working memory profiles — executive versus phonological deficits, respectively. What does this selectivity suggest about the independence of working memory components, and what are the implications for differential diagnosis and targeted intervention?
> 2. Normal aging disproportionately impairs central executive functions while sparing simpler phonological and visuospatial capacities. What compensatory cognitive strategies or environmental designs would most effectively offset age-related working memory decline?
> 3. Neuroimaging reveals that older adults recruit prefrontal resources bilaterally for tasks that younger adults handle unilaterally. Is this compensatory recruitment a success (similar performance preserved at higher neural cost) or a sign of vulnerability (more effort required for the same output)? What evidence would resolve this?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Given that working memory capacity is approximately four chunks, and that chunks can be of arbitrary complexity for experts, what does this imply about the optimal level of detail for a note in a PKM system?
> 2. How does recognizing that long-term memory is practically unlimited but working memory is severely limited change your intuitions about *where* the value of a PKM system actually lies?
> 3. If automaticity transfers processing from working to long-term memory, what PKM practices might accelerate automaticity development in your primary domains of study?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. If germane load is not truly separable from intrinsic processing, how does this affect the prescriptive value of CLT for instructional design? Does the revised understanding make the theory stronger or weaker as a design framework?
> 2. The evolutionary argument distinguishes biologically primary from biologically secondary knowledge. What does this imply about which PKM practices might be most cognitively natural versus which require the most deliberate design?
> 3. Think of a topic you are currently learning. Using element interactivity analysis, rank its major sub-components by their interactivity. Does the typical order in which these are taught match what CLT would predict is optimal?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Identify a note you have written recently. Does it create split-attention effects by requiring navigation to multiple separate sources before its core claim is comprehensible? How might you redesign it?
> 2. The worked-example principle suggests that capturing reasoning processes (not just conclusions) in notes has pedagogical value. How does your current note-making practice balance conclusion-statements against process-traces?
> 3. How should a PKM system "know" when to show a practitioner more scaffolded templates versus less? Is metadata-based expertise tracking feasible?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Does your current PKM practice function as an externalized archive, an externalized working memory, or an externalized long-term memory? Are these roles in tension, and how do you manage the tension?
> 2. The Zettelkasten principle of writing for a "future self" represents a design choice to preserve both context and connections. How does the fidelity of that contextual preservation relate to CLT's concepts of intrinsic load and element interactivity?
> 3. Where in your AI-assisted PKM practices is the boundary between appropriate cognitive offloading and problematic schema-formation-bypass? Is that boundary clear, and how do you enforce it?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Audit your last ten notes using the CLT-PKM Alignment Framework. Which phase are they primarily products of? Does their load topology reflect the phase they belong to?
> 2. If annotated linking is more cognitively valuable than unannotated linking, does this change how you approach the time investment in link creation? What is the threshold below which annotation effort generates more extraneous than germane load?
> 3. How does your spaced repetition practice (if any) currently interface with your note-making practice? Is retrieval practice drawing on the same material as your notes, or are these systems isolated?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Which of the six design principles does your current PKM system most clearly violate? What would remediation look like, and what would the cognitive cost of redesign be?
> 2. Dual-channel representation requires comfort with visual/diagrammatic note forms. If you do not currently use diagrams in your PKM, what is the barrier — skill, tooling, habit, or philosophical objection?
> 3. Design a spaced repetition protocol for a specific knowledge domain you are currently developing in your PKM. How would you create retrieval cards, schedule reviews, and integrate this with your existing note-making workflow?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Have you ever found that a more complex PKM system generated more cognitive overhead than it saved? What specific design features contributed to that outcome?
> 2. How do you currently test whether your PKM practices are producing biological memory schemas rather than merely well-organized external storage? Do you have any form of outcome measurement?
> 3. How confident are you in your own metacognitive monitoring of cognitive load states? What would it take to improve that calibration?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. In what ways does framing instructional design as a "cognitive resource allocation problem" change how you evaluate teaching methods you have experienced?
> 2. Why might conventional problem solving impose higher extraneous load than studying worked examples, even though problem solving feels more "active"?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. How does the schema-based account of expertise challenge the common assumption that experts "simply know more" than novices?
> 2. Consider a domain you are developing expertise in. Can you identify schemas you have constructed that now reduce the cognitive load of tasks that once felt overwhelming?
> 3. What are the implications of the biologically primary/secondary distinction for debates about "natural" versus "instructed" learning?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Given the germane load reconceptualization, is the original three-factor model still useful as a design heuristic, even if theoretically imprecise?
> 2. Can you identify a learning experience where extraneous load from poor instructional design compounded the difficulty of inherently complex material?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. How would you assess the element interactivity of a topic you are currently learning? What elements must be held simultaneously in working memory?
> 2. What are the practical implications of the expertise reversal effect for differentiated instruction in classrooms containing learners at varying expertise levels?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Consider instructional materials you regularly use. Which CLT effects do they violate, and how might they be redesigned?
> 2. How does the expertise reversal effect challenge the common educational goal of creating a "best" curriculum?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. If cognitive load cannot be reliably decomposed into its theorized components, what are the implications for CLT's status as a scientific theory?
> 2. How might advances in neuroimaging or real-time physiological monitoring address the measurement-theory gap?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. Is the reconceptualization of germane load a theoretical retreat or a theoretical improvement? What criteria would help you decide?
> 2. How might the distinction between biologically primary and secondary knowledge change how you approach learning different subjects?
> 3. Can you identify a learning experience where "productive failure" preceded understanding? How does this relate to CLT?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> 1. In what areas of your own learning or professional practice would CLT principles be most directly applicable?
> 2. How might the dynamic load optimization perspective change the way educational technology is designed?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. When you read an academic paper in an unfamiliar field, which level of representation do you typically construct? What signals tell you when you have achieved a genuine situation model rather than merely a textbase?
> 2. How often, in your academic reading practice, do you consciously generate elaborative inferences — pausing to ask *why*, *how*, or *what does this imply*? How might increasing this practice change what you retain?
> 3. The distinction between textbase and situation model maps onto the common experience of "knowing" something for an exam versus "understanding" it well enough to use it. What structural differences in your reading process might explain this gap?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. Think of a recent experience of reading academic text at the limits of your working memory capacity. What strategies did you deploy, and were they effective at managing load or did they create additional load?
> 2. In which academic domains do you have rich schemas that accelerate comprehension, and in which are schemas sparse? How does this architectural difference manifest in your experience of reading in those domains?
> 3. The Matthew Effect implies that the most costly educational investment is often domain knowledge building rather than strategy instruction. Does this accord with your experience of what "improves your reading" in unfamiliar fields?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. How would you characterize your current comprehension monitoring accuracy? What evidence from situations where you thought you understood something and later discovered you didn't gives you insight into the gaps between your monitoring and actual comprehension?
> 2. Conditional metacognitive knowledge — knowing *when* to use *which* strategy — is the hardest to develop. What experiences have most developed this meta-level knowledge for you, and were they designed instructional experiences or incidental ones?
> 3. The sub-threshold monitoring insight suggests that developing metacognitive skill may involve cultivating sensitivity to early, weak signals rather than simply attending more carefully to obvious confusion. How might you restructure your reading practice to create better access to these early signals?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. In which academic disciplines do you have robust genre schemas — can you predict the structural moves of a text before reading it? In which are you reading without these schemas? How does this difference manifest in your comprehension experience?
> 2. The concept of "modal shift" to discursive reading mode implies that you actively choose how to read. How often do you make this conscious mode selection, versus defaulting to whatever reading mode is habitual?
> 3. High-frequency academic vocabulary (the Academic Word List tier) is more broadly learnable than technical vocabulary. What systematic approaches to this vocabulary might be worth developing?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. Review your current academic reading practices honestly. What proportion of your strategy use falls into "high utility" (retrieval practice, elaborative interrogation, self-explanation) versus "low utility" (re-reading, passive highlighting) categories? What would it take to shift this distribution?
> 2. Self-explanation requires approaching difficult passages as opportunities for explanation rather than obstacles to avoid. How do you currently respond to passages you find opaque? What would a "self-explanation habit" look like in practice in your reading?
> 3. The claim that strategy selection is the master skill implies that the most important thing to develop may not be mastery of any single strategy but fluency in selecting across a repertoire. How would you design a reading practice that develops this meta-strategic flexibility?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. The prior knowledge paradox — needing to know something to learn from texts about it — is not an insuperable barrier but rather a bootstrapping challenge. What strategies have you found most effective for developing entry-level schemas in genuinely new academic domains?
> 2. The Matthew Effect-metacognitive interaction trap predicts that your comprehension monitoring will be least reliable precisely where you most need it — in new domains with limited prior knowledge. How might you design reading practices that compensate for this predictable metacognitive gap?
> 3. If domain vocabulary is the primary access route to domain schemas, what would a deliberate vocabulary-building practice in a new academic field look like? How would it differ from casual vocabulary accumulation through reading?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. The scaffolding model of instruction implies that the goal is not to provide permanent support but to gradually eliminate it as competence develops. Applied to your own reading development, where are you in the I-We-You progression for different types of academic texts? What forms of external scaffolding are you currently depending on that you want to gradually internalize?
> 2. Metacognitive instruction should precede or accompany strategy instruction, not follow it. Reflecting on strategy instruction you have received or practiced: how often was this sequencing followed? What would it look like to re-sequence your own self-directed learning to follow this principle?
> 3. Zimmerman's self-reflection phase — evaluating outcomes and attributing difficulties to appropriate causes — is what converts reading experiences into metacognitive learning. How systematically do you engage in this phase after completing difficult academic reading?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> 1. The profile of the metacognitive lifelong learner describes a reader who has internalized comprehensive reading-regulation capabilities. Honestly evaluating yourself against this profile: what are the two or three dimensions where you are currently strongest, and what are the two or three where you have the most development work to do?
> 2. The Fluency Illusion — mistaking processing ease for comprehension depth — is particularly insidious for lifelong learners revisiting familiar texts. When you re-read foundational texts in your areas of expertise, how do you protect against this? Do you test comprehension depth or mostly trust the familiar feeling of understanding?
> 3. Writing to learn treats every reading experience as the beginning of a knowledge construction project, not its endpoint. What would it look like to design your academic reading practice so that writing (synthesis, explanation, connection-making) is normatively expected after significant reading, not merely an occasional activity?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. Think of a recent communication failure you experienced. Was the primary problem at the level of verbal clarity, schema mismatch, relational framing, or cognitive bias?
> 2. How does the schema-dependence of communication change your approach to explaining something to someone who knows far less about the topic than you do?
> 3. In what contexts do you most systematically fall into confirmation bias as a listener? What communicative conditions trigger it?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. In your own habitual communication contexts, what is the main source of extraneous cognitive load you impose on your audience?
> 2. How would you explain your area of expertise to a curious, intelligent 14-year-old? What would you have to give up, and what would you discover you could keep?
> 3. What signposting habits could you develop that would most improve the clarity of your messages in high-stakes communication situations?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. Which of the three listening levels do you typically operate at in professional conversations? In personal conflicts? In conversations with people you disagree with strongly?
> 2. What is your characteristic listening failure mode: internal monologue, rebuttal machine, or confirmation bias filtering? What triggers it most reliably?
> 3. When was the last time someone demonstrated to you — through reflective paraphrase or genuine questioning — that they had truly understood your position? How did it affect the quality of the conversation?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. What nonverbal habits do you have that might be sending unintended signals in professional or personal communication contexts? How would you even know?
> 2. Think of a communicator whose nonverbal presence you find particularly persuasive or trustworthy. What specific nonverbal qualities account for that impression?
> 3. In what communicative contexts are you most prone to missing nonverbal cues — rushing past them, misinterpreting them, or not attending to them at all?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. In what conversations do you most reliably shift to solution mode before adequately acknowledging the emotional dimension? What drives that pattern?
> 2. What is the psychological safety level in your primary communication contexts? What specific communicative behaviors are being systematically suppressed?
> 3. When someone challenges you directly, what is your characteristic first-response emotional pattern, and how does it affect the quality of what you say next?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. Think about the feedback you typically give. At which level does it typically operate — task, process, self-regulation, or self? What would it look like to shift one level up?
> 2. In your professional communication, what proportion of time do you spend in advocacy mode versus genuine inquiry mode? What would change if you shifted the ratio?
> 3. Can you identify a conversation in your life that genuinely functioned as dialogue — where both parties were changed by thinking together? What conditions made that possible?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. In your own communication, where do you most commonly deploy informal fallacies — even subtly? What are the argumentative shortcuts you most rely on?
> 2. Think about someone whose persuasive communication you find particularly credible. What is the balance of logos, ethos, and pathos? What specifically makes their ethos convincing?
> 3. How do you calibrate the tension between advocating your genuine position persuasively and respecting your audience's epistemic autonomy?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> 1. What are the two or three digital communication environments you operate in most? What are the distinctive norms, affordances, and failure modes of each?
> 2. How has your information consumption through digital platforms shaped the epistemic environment your thinking occurs in? What confirmation biases might it be reinforcing?
> 3. Have you experienced a cross-cultural communication failure that, in retrospect, you understand differently now? What cultural communication framework does it illustrate?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. Have you ever spent more cognitive effort navigating your PKM system than you would have spent simply thinking through the problem unaided? What specific features of the system were responsible?
> 2. The productivity industry rarely measures cognitive cost — it measures perceived productivity. How would you design an honest cost-benefit accounting for your current knowledge management approach?
> 3. If you had to identify the single most cognitively expensive element of your PKM system right now, what would it be?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. Reflect on a domain in which you are genuinely expert. How does your experience of "effortlessness" in that domain relate to schema formation — and how might your PKM system either support or interfere with equivalent schema development in areas where you are not yet expert?
> 2. Sweller's key insight was that students can work hard on a task without learning from it. Can you identify PKM workflows where you expend genuine effort without building any durable cognitive structure?
> 3. If your PKM practice were evaluated as an instructional design — by its effectiveness at building schemas rather than producing output — how would it score?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. Can you recall a specific moment of genuine germane processing in your PKM practice — a moment of intellectual synthesis, unexpected connection, or schema formation that felt productive and intrinsically motivated? What design features of your system enabled that moment?
> 2. Of the six extraneous load categories, which three are most prevalent in your system? What would it cost (in time, in lost functionality) to address each?
> 3. The additive constraint suggests that even good individual features can combine badly. Can you identify a combination of simultaneous demands in your own PKM workflow that routinely exceeds your capacity?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. Which component of your working memory is most consistently overloaded during PKM work — the Central Executive (managing competing demands), the phonological loop (processing verbal load), or the episodic buffer (attempting synthesis with insufficient resources)?
> 2. Think about the last time a note in your system genuinely triggered a useful memory that you wouldn't have had otherwise. What design features of that note made it a successful retrieval cue?
> 3. Have you noticed a "digital amnesia" effect in your own information retention — remembering that you have something captured but not what it contains? What does this tell you about your current offloading strategy?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. Which of the five failure modes is most recognisable in your experience — either in your own system or in systems you've tried to maintain? What triggered the collapse?
> 2. The synthesis debt concept implies that there's an optimal capture/synthesis ratio for each practitioner. What would you estimate that ratio to be for your work, and how does your current practice compare?
> 3. If you were redesigning your PKM system with a strict "minimum viable structure" constraint, what would you eliminate first? What would you absolutely retain?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. Can you identify a specific knowledge domain where your PKM system supports progressive navigation (from foundational to synthesis-level notes) and one where it doesn't? What's the difference?
> 2. How do you currently manage the experience of entering a new high-interactivity knowledge domain where you have few relevant schemas? Do you have explicit strategies, or is it largely ad-hoc?
> 3. The claim that "intrinsic load cannot be reduced by design" has an important corollary: time and effort are required to develop the schemas that reduce intrinsic load through expertise. What are the implications of this for sustainable PKM practice at scale?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. In your PKM practice, roughly what proportion of your time is spent in activities that require generative, connection-building effort versus activities that feel smooth and productive but may be shallow? Is that ratio appropriate?
> 2. Have you experienced the generation effect in your own note-writing — a situation where attempting to write from memory before checking your sources produced noticeably better retention? What prompted you to work that way?
> 3. How do you currently handle the gap between captured information and consolidated knowledge in your PKM system? If you don't have an explicit mechanism, what would it cost to add one?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. At which stage of PKM expertise development would you place yourself overall? Are there specific domains within your system where you are at a different stage than your general level would suggest?
> 2. Can you identify a PKM structure you maintain that was essential when you set it up but now feels more like overhead than support? What would it cost to simplify or retire it?
> 3. Have you experienced PKM framework fatigue — the impulse to start over with a new method when the current one starts generating friction? With the expertise-reversal framing in mind, how do you interpret those prior experiences?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. If you use one of these three methods (or have tried one), how accurately does the CLT analysis capture your experience of its costs and benefits? What does the analysis miss?
> 2. The claim that "no single method addresses both extraneous load reduction and germane load promotion" suggests that hybrid approaches are likely optimal. What would an ideal hybrid look like for your specific knowledge work profile?
> 3. The expertise-reversal framing suggests that each method has an optimal practitioner profile. Does the profile described for each method match the populations you've seen using it successfully?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> 1. The Load-Phase Model implies that the most cognitively important phases (Processing and Synthesis) are the most poorly supported by the design decisions most PKM practitioners focus on. Does this match your experience?
> 2. If you adopted only one of the four system-level principles — simplicity, decision separation, retrieval-path orientation, or mandatory connections — which would produce the greatest immediate improvement in your system?
> 3. The minimum viable system description trades power and granularity for cognitive economy. What would you need to give up from your current system to approach this minimum? Would you be willing to make that trade?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. If working memory is defined by active processing rather than passive storage, what does this imply about the adequacy of metaphors that describe memory as a "container" or "filing cabinet"?
> 2. How does the bidirectional relationship between working memory and long-term memory complicate simple models of information flow in cognition?
> 3. In what sense might working memory's severe capacity constraints be understood not as a limitation but as a condition that shapes the distinctive character of human thought?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. Why was the shift from "short-term memory" to "working memory" more than a change in terminology? What theoretical commitments does the newer term encode?
> 2. How did the dual-task paradigm provide evidence against the unitary-buffer model, and what architectural conclusions does selective interference support?
> 3. What role did the [[Levels-of-Processing|levels-of-processing]] framework play in creating the theoretical space for the working memory concept?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. How does the phonological similarity effect provide evidence specifically for a sound-based storage code, and what would have to be true of the storage code for this effect to disappear?
> 2. In what sense does the episodic buffer resolve the tension between working memory's limited capacity and the apparently large capacity demonstrated in prose recall?
> 3. If the central executive is decomposed into updating, shifting, and inhibition, what does the "common EF" factor that underlies all three suggest about the nature of executive control?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. If Cowan's model is correct and working memory is simply the activated portion of long-term memory, what becomes of the explanatory role played by dedicated stores like the phonological loop?
> 2. How does Engle's reframing of working memory capacity as attentional control change the predictions one would make about training and intervention programmes?
> 3. Is the debate between multicomponent and embedded-processes models genuinely about architecture, or is it better understood as a debate about the appropriate level of description for the same underlying phenomena?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. If the true capacity of working memory is closer to four items, how does this revised estimate change the instructional design implications compared to Miller's estimate of seven?
> 2. What does the expertise-driven expansion of effective capacity through chunking imply about the relationship between working memory and knowledge?
> 3. If working memory training does not reliably improve fluid intelligence, what does this suggest about the malleability of the cognitive architecture?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. How does the distributed-network view of working memory's neural implementation challenge or complement the modular architecture proposed by the multicomponent model?
> 2. What does the "last in, first out" pattern of prefrontal development and decline imply about the design of educational and clinical interventions?
> 3. In what ways does the hippocampal contribution to working memory binding blur the classical boundary between short-term and long-term memory systems?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. How does the concept of element interactivity resolve the apparent paradox that the same material can be easy for one learner and overwhelming for another?
> 2. In what sense is cognitive load theory an "applied theory of working memory" rather than merely a set of design heuristics?
> 3. What are the implications of the expertise reversal effect for adaptive instructional systems that must serve learners at different levels of development?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> 1. How does the metacognitive overhead problem illuminate the difficulty that novice learners experience with self-regulation, and what instructional approaches might address this difficulty?
> 2. If working memory is the "common currency" of higher cognition, what are the implications for educational programmes that aim to improve cognitive performance through domain-general training?
> 3. In what sense does the relationship between expertise and metacognition suggest that metacognitive development is not purely a matter of skill instruction but also of domain knowledge acquisition?






































































































## Spaced Repetition Seeds

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Working Memory
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between working memory and short-term memory, and why does the distinction matter?
> **A:** Short-term memory refers to any passive temporary storage of information; working memory additionally involves active manipulation as well as multiple specialized components (phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, central executive, episodic buffer). The distinction matters because simple digit-span measures of short-term memory fail to predict higher cognitive functioning, while complex span measures of working memory (which engage executive attention) strongly predict reasoning, comprehension, and learning outcomes.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Phonological Loop Phenomena
> **A:** **Q:** Name and briefly explain the four core phenomena demonstrating the phonological loop's architecture.
> **A:** (1) *Phonological similarity effect*: phonologically similar sequences are harder to recall (shows phonological coding). (2) *Word-length effect*: fewer long words recalled than short words (shows rehearsal buffer time-limited). (3) *Irrelevant speech effect*: background speech impairs verbal recall even when ignored (shows automatic access to phonological store). (4) *Articulatory suppression*: speaking concurrently eliminates word-length effect and disrupts phonological coding (shows rehearsal's dependence on articulatory motor programming).

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Slave Systems vs. Central Executive
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fundamental distinction between the slave systems (phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad) and the central executive in Baddeley's model?
> **A:** Slave systems are dedicated storage and maintenance buffers for specific representational formats (phonological/verbal; visuospatial). They operate with relative automaticity once activated. The central executive is a domain-general, limited-capacity attentional control system with no dedicated storage — it manages attention, coordinates slave systems, inhibits irrelevant information, and interfaces with long-term memory. It is the executive control that makes working memory "working."

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Process: Cognitive Load Reduction
> **A:** **Q:** According to Cognitive Load Theory, what is the primary goal of instructional design, and what is the mechanism linking it to learning?
> **A:** The primary goal is to reduce *extraneous cognitive load* (load imposed by poor instructional format) while preserving *germane cognitive load* (effort invested in schema construction). When extraneous load is reduced, working memory executive resources are freed for the active integration and elaboration of new information into long-term schemas — which is the mechanism of learning. Without freed executive capacity, the learner processes the material but cannot invest cognitive effort in schema formation.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application: Split-Attention Effect
> **A:** **Q:** What is the split-attention effect and what instructional design principle does it motivate?
> **A:** The split-attention effect is the finding that learners who must mentally integrate information from physically or temporally separated sources perform worse than those who process the same information in an integrated format. The mechanism is that mental integration itself consumes working memory executive resources, leaving less capacity for learning. The design principle is *spatial and temporal contiguity*: place related text and diagrams physically adjacent (spatial contiguity) and present complementary narration and animation simultaneously rather than sequentially (temporal contiguity).

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Near vs. Far Transfer in WM Training
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between near and far transfer in the context of working memory training, and what does the evidence show?
> **A:** *Near transfer*: improvement on tasks similar to the trained tasks (e.g., train on one working memory span task, improve on other span tasks). *Far transfer*: improvement on untrained, conceptually distinct cognitive abilities (reading comprehension, mathematics, fluid intelligence). Evidence: near transfer is consistently found in WM training studies; far transfer is generally *not* found in well-controlled conditions (Melby-Lervåg & Hulme, 2013). The implication: WM training improves WM task performance without expanding the broader cognitive capacity associated with WM.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Connection: Episodic Buffer and Reading Comprehension
> **A:** **Q:** How does the episodic buffer explain the comprehension of complex narratives that exceed the capacity of the phonological loop or visuospatial sketchpad alone?
> **A:** Complex narrative comprehension requires integrating verbal content (phonological loop), physical scene descriptions (visuospatial sketchpad), semantic knowledge (long-term memory), and temporal/causal structure into a unified situation model. The episodic buffer provides the temporary multimodal integration workspace where this binding occurs — it can hold approximately 4 chunks of integrated multimodal information, where each "chunk" is a rich episodic representation combining verbal, visual, spatial, and semantic features. Without the episodic buffer, the model cannot explain how working memory maintains complex situation models during reading.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application: Working Memory in ADHD vs. Dyslexia
> **A:** **Q:** How do the working memory profiles in ADHD and dyslexia differ, and what does this selectivity reveal about working memory architecture?
> **A:** ADHD: selective impairment in central executive functions (controlled attention, updating, inhibition, task-switching) with relatively preserved simple phonological span. Dyslexia: selective impairment in phonological loop capacity (reduced digit span, impaired non-word repetition, low phonological awareness) with relatively preserved visuospatial and executive functions. The selectivity demonstrates the independence of working memory components: ADHD implicates prefrontal executive systems; dyslexia implicates left inferior parietal/Broca's area phonological systems. Each disorder validates the model's architectural distinctions.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Expertise Reversal Effect
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise reversal effect and why does it challenge the universal application of CLT instructional principles?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect: instructional guidance that helps novices learn can actually impair learning in more advanced learners. As expertise grows, long-term schemas reduce the intrinsic load of the material, and scaffolding becomes redundant and forces processing of information the expert already knows — imposing extraneous load without benefit. This challenges universal application of CLT principles because the optimal instructional format depends on learner expertise: worked examples for novices; problem-solving for experts; faded guidance for learners transitioning between these stages.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Baddeley vs. Cowan Models
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fundamental architectural difference between Baddeley's multicomponent model and Cowan's embedded-processes model?
> **A:** Baddeley: proposes specialized short-term storage subsystems (phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad) that are structurally distinct from long-term memory, with capacity determined by modality-specific mechanisms. Cowan: proposes working memory is simply activated long-term memory — a subset of activated representations held in a limited focus of attention (~3-4 chunks, modality-independent). No specialized short-term stores; just different activation levels of long-term memory. Key difference: Baddeley predicts modality-specific capacity limits; Cowan predicts a single modality-independent capacity limit for the focus of attention.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Process: Central Executive Functions
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four principal functions of the central executive in Baddeley's model?
> **A:** (1) *Focusing attention*: selecting relevant information from competing inputs and maintaining goal-relevant representations. (2) *Dividing attention*: distributing attentional resources across multiple concurrent tasks. (3) *Switching attention*: flexibly shifting between task sets, retrieval strategies, or mental representations (with associated switching costs). (4) *Interfacing with long-term memory*: selectively activating relevant long-term knowledge to support current working memory operations, enabling reasoning and comprehension tasks that require integration of stored knowledge with new information.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application: Modality Effect in Multimedia Learning
> **A:** **Q:** What is the modality effect in multimedia learning, how does Baddeley's model explain it, and what is its design implication?
> **A:** The modality effect: learning is better when complex visual material is accompanied by spoken narration rather than on-screen text. Baddeley's explanation: text and diagram compete for the same visuospatial processing channel, causing bottleneck. Narration and diagram use separate channels (phonological loop for narration; visuospatial sketchpad for diagram), allowing parallel processing and more total information capacity. Design implication: for complex animated or diagrammatic material, use spoken narration rather than on-screen explanatory text to exploit the dual-channel architecture of working memory.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the core claim of CLT's prescription for instruction?
> **Answer:** Instructional design should minimize extraneous cognitive load (load generated by poor design) and ensure that intrinsic load is directed toward schema formation, because working memory capacity is severely limited and must not be wasted on processing that does not produce long-term memory schemas.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** CLT, working-memory, instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Distinguish intrinsic from extraneous cognitive load. Give a PKM example of each.
> **Answer:** Intrinsic load arises from the inherent complexity of the material (element interactivity); it cannot be eliminated without reducing content complexity. Extraneous load arises from poor design that does not contribute to schema formation; it can and should be minimized. PKM example of intrinsic: understanding a complex philosophical argument that requires holding many interrelated premises simultaneously. PKM example of extraneous: having to navigate between five different notes to assemble information that could be co-located in one atomic note.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** CLT, intrinsic-load, extraneous-load, PKM

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the expertise-reversal effect, and what does it imply for PKM template design?
> **Answer:** The expertise-reversal effect is the phenomenon whereby instructional scaffolding that reduces cognitive load for novices becomes redundant and counterproductive load for experts who have internalized the relevant schemas. For PKM template design, it implies that scaffolding and elaboration that helps beginners structure their notes should be progressively reduced as the practitioner's expertise in a domain grows; static templates that never evolve will generate redundancy load for the expert practitioner.
> **Source:** Sections 3.5, 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** expertise-reversal, PKM, template-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Define the split-attention effect and describe how atomic note design addresses it.
> **Answer:** The split-attention effect is the performance/learning decrement from having to simultaneously attend to and mentally integrate multiple spatially or temporally separated sources of mutually dependent information. Atomic note design addresses it by ensuring that each note is self-contained with respect to its central claim: all information necessary to understand the claim is co-located within the note, so the reader does not need to simultaneously consult multiple sources to understand any single claim.
> **Source:** Sections 3.2, 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** split-attention, atomic-notes, PKM

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the Load Topology of PKM Workflows?
> **Answer:** An original framework (this report) that maps PKM workflow phases (Capture → Structure → Connect → Retrieve) to their dominant load type and risk, and the corresponding optimal load management strategy. Capture: risk of extraneous load from simultaneous transcription + comprehension; strategy: selective abstraction with deliberate delay. Structure: risk of intrinsic load from 과high element-interactivity notes; strategy: atomicity. Connect: risk of extraneous from automated/unannotated links; strategy: annotated self-explanation linking. Retrieve: risk of passive re-reading; strategy: spaced retrieval practice.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** CLT, PKM, original-framework, load-topology

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Why does the retrieval practice effect produce superior memory consolidation compared to passive re-reading, in CLT terms?
> **Answer:** Retrieval practice generates a distinct pattern of cognitive reactivation: reconstructing information from memory requires schema activation and application, generating the moderate-difficulty germane processing that consolidates memory traces. Passive re-reading merely re-exposes the brain to the same input without requiring reactivation of the stored schema, producing recognition without consolidation. In CLT terms, retrieval practice generates productive germane load (schema reactivation and strengthening) while passive re-reading generates low-benefit processing that mimics encoding without the consolidation-generating cognitive effort.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** retrieval-practice, germane-load, PKM, spaced-repetition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is cognitive offloading, and when is it adaptive vs. maladaptive in PKM practice?
> **Answer:** Cognitive offloading is using external resources to perform cognitive operations that would otherwise be performed internally, reducing working memory demands. In PKM: adaptive when the offloaded operation (storing a reference, recording a fact for later retrieval) would impose excessive WM load without contributing to schema formation — the external storage frees WM for the schema-building processing that matters. Maladaptive when it substitutes for internal processing that produces durable schemas: using AI to summarize instead of reading and reformulating eliminates the germane processing that would have formed the schema.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** cognitive-offloading, PKM, schema-formation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the CLT-PKM Alignment Framework, and what are its four phases?
> **Answer:** An original two-dimensional framework (this report) integrating CLT effects with PKM workflow phases. Four phases: (1) Capture — risk: extraneous load from simultaneous tasks; mechanism: elaborative encoding through reformulation; strategy: selective capture + delayed processing. (2) Structure — risk: intrinsic load from high element-interactivity notes; mechanism: chunking through well-scoped note boundaries; strategy: atomicity. (3) Connect — risk: extraneous load from automated/unannotated links; mechanism: relational schema construction; strategy: annotated linking with self-explanation rationale. (4) Retrieve — risk: passive re-reading without consolidation; mechanism: schema strengthening through reactivation; strategy: spaced retrieval practice.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** CLT-PKM-alignment, original-framework, PKM

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What does the modality effect recommend for PKM note design, and what is its boundary condition?
> **Answer:** The modality effect recommends that PKM notes on complex, high-element-interactivity material should integrate verbal and visual representations (diagrams, concept maps, timelines) because verbal-visual integration distributes processing across two working memory subsystems (phonological loop + visuospatial sketchpad), effectively expanding available processing capacity. Boundary condition: the benefit holds only when both representations carry complementary (non-redundant) information. A diagram that merely illustrates what the adjacent text already states creates redundancy load rather than load-distribution benefit.
> **Source:** Sections 3.4, 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** modality-effect, dual-coding, PKM-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** What is Cognitive Load Theory?
> **A:** **Q:** What is Cognitive Load Theory, and what is its central claim?
> **A:** CLT is an instructional design theory arguing that learning is optimized when instructional procedures accommodate the limitations of human cognitive architecture — specifically, the limited capacity of working memory and the organizational role of schemas in long-term memory. Its central claim is that instructional design should manage cognitive load by reducing extraneous demands and directing processing toward schema construction.
> **Source:** Sections 1-2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #clt-definition, #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Three Types of Cognitive Load
> **A:** **Q:** Name and define the three types of cognitive load in CLT's original framework.
> **A:** (1) *Intrinsic load* — inherent complexity of the material determined by element interactivity relative to learner expertise; cannot be altered by design. (2) *Extraneous load* — unnecessary load imposed by poor instructional design; always detrimental and always reducible. (3) *Germane load* — working memory resources devoted to schema construction; later reconceptualized as not an independent source but a functional description of resources allocated to processing intrinsic load.
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #load-types, #intrinsic, #extraneous, #germane

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Element Interactivity
> **A:** **Q:** What is element interactivity, and why is it central to CLT?
> **A:** Element interactivity is the degree to which elements of a learning task must be processed simultaneously because they are logically interdependent. It is central because it determines intrinsic cognitive load: high interactivity means more elements must be held and integrated in working memory simultaneously. It is also expertise-relative — experts chunk elements via schemas, reducing effective interactivity.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #element-interactivity, #intrinsic-load, #mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Worked Example vs. Problem Solving
> **A:** **Q:** Why do worked examples produce better learning than problem-solving practice for novices, according to CLT?
> **A:** Conventional problem-solving requires means-ends analysis — simultaneously maintaining goal state, current state, operators, and subgoals — which imposes heavy extraneous load that does not contribute to schema construction. Worked examples eliminate means-ends analysis, allowing learners to devote working memory to understanding the solution rationale, which promotes schema construction.
> **Source:** Section 5.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #worked-example-effect, #means-ends-analysis, #schema-construction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Expertise Reversal Effect
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise reversal effect, and what is its key implication for instructional design?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect is the finding that instructional techniques effective for novices (e.g., worked examples, integrated formats) become ineffective or counterproductive for advanced learners. The key implication is that no single instructional approach is universally optimal — effective CLT-based design must be adaptive, adjusting support to learner expertise level.
> **Source:** Sections 4, 5.5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #expertise-reversal, #adaptive-instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Split Attention vs. Redundancy
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between the split attention effect and the redundancy effect?
> **A:** Split attention occurs when learners must mentally integrate two *mutually referring* sources that cannot be understood in isolation (solution: physically integrate them). Redundancy occurs when two sources are *self-contained* — each can be understood independently, so the additional source adds processing cost without information value (solution: remove the redundant source). The key distinction: mutually referring sources need integration; self-contained sources need elimination.
> **Source:** Sections 5.2, 5.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #split-attention, #redundancy, #design-principle

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Germane Load Reconceptualization
> **A:** **Q:** How did Sweller (2010) reconceptualize germane cognitive load, and why?
> **A:** Sweller argued that germane load is not an independent load source parallel to intrinsic and extraneous load, but rather the working memory resources devoted to processing intrinsic load when extraneous load has been minimized. The reason: germane load cannot be independently measured or manipulated apart from intrinsic load, making it unfalsifiable as a separate construct. The reconceptualized model has only two independent sources: intrinsic and extraneous.
> **Source:** Section 7.1
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #germane-load, #reconceptualization, #theoretical-debate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** CLT and Desirable Difficulties
> **A:** **Q:** How is the apparent tension between CLT (reduce load) and desirable difficulties (add difficulty) resolved?
> **A:** Desirable difficulties increase *intrinsic* processing demands — they require deeper engagement with the material's inherent structure (e.g., retrieval practice forces schema reconstruction, interleaving forces discrimination). They do not add extraneous load. CLT predicts these should enhance learning when total load remains within working memory capacity, which the evidence confirms — with the caveat that desirable difficulties fail when intrinsic load is already high.
> **Source:** Section 7.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties, #reconciliation, #intrinsic-load

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Productive Failure
> **A:** **Q:** What is productive failure, and how does it challenge and extend CLT?
> **A:** Productive failure (Kapur) involves learners first attempting problems without instruction (and failing), then receiving direct instruction. It often outperforms instruction-first approaches. It challenges CLT's prediction that novices should receive guidance first, but extends the theory by demonstrating that pre-instruction failure activates prior knowledge, reveals gaps, and creates "knowledge differentiation" that makes subsequent instruction more effective. It suggests CLT needs temporal framing — optimizing across learning sequences, not just individual events.
> **Source:** Section 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #productive-failure, #boundary-condition, #sequence-effects

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Working Memory Capacity Estimates
> **A:** **Q:** What are the key estimates of working memory capacity relevant to CLT, and who proposed them?
> **A:** George Miller (1956) proposed "7 ± 2" items, but this includes rehearsed and chunked items. Nelson Cowan (2001) argued that *pure* working memory capacity — controlling for rehearsal and chunking — is approximately 4 chunks. CLT adopts the more conservative estimate, as it follows directly that high element interactivity (>4 novel interacting elements) will exceed capacity for novices.
> **Source:** Section 2.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory, #capacity, #miller, #cowan

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the difference between a textbase and a situation model in reading comprehension?
> **A:** **A:** A *textbase* is a propositional semantic representation of what the text explicitly says — a network of propositions derived from the text's surface content. A *situation model* is a richer mental model of the actual situation, event, or concept described by the text, incorporating prior knowledge, inferences, spatial relationships, and causal structure. Situation models support transfer and problem-solving; textbase representations support paraphrase and recognition.
> **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #reading-comprehension #cognitive-science

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Fluency Illusion, and why does it undermine metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** **A:** The Fluency Illusion is the systematic tendency to interpret *ease of text processing* (perceptual fluency) as evidence of *comprehension depth*, when fluency and comprehension are not reliably related. Re-reading increases fluency without necessarily improving situation model quality — but the increased fluency feels like improved understanding. This creates false metacognitive confidence and is a primary driver of the passive re-reading trap.
> **Source:** Section 8.2 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #metacognition #fluency-illusion

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What are the three sub-types of metacognitive knowledge, and which is most predictive of strategic reading proficiency?
> **A:** **A:** (1) *Declarative* — knowing *what* strategies exist; (2) *Procedural* — knowing *how* to execute strategies; (3) *Conditional* — knowing *when* and *why* to use which strategy. Conditional metacognitive knowledge is most predictive of advanced reading proficiency and hardest to develop through instruction.
> **Source:** Section 3.1 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #metacognition #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Matthew Effect in reading, and what does this report claim about its interaction with metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** **A:** The Matthew Effect describes how early reading advantages (vocabulary, comprehension) compound over time — rich get richer, poor get poorer in literacy development. This report's synthesis argues that the Matthew Effect creates a *double failure trap*: poor domain knowledge simultaneously degrades comprehension (less effective schema application) and degrades metacognitive monitoring accuracy (schemas needed for monitoring failures are absent). Both deficits compound each other.
> **Source:** Section 6.4 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #matthew-effect #metacognition #prior-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** According to Dunlosky et al. (2013), which learning strategies have high utility? Which have low utility?
> **A:** **A:** **High utility:** retrieval practice (practice testing) and spaced repetition. **Moderate utility:** elaborative interrogation, self-explanation, interleaving. **Low utility:** re-reading and highlighting. The high-utility strategies are underused by most students despite being most effective; the low-utility strategies are overused because they feel effective (due to Fluency Illusion).
> **Source:** Section 5.2 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #learning-strategies #retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is Reciprocal Teaching, and what four strategies does it involve?
> **A:** **A:** Reciprocal Teaching (Palincsar & Brown, 1984) is a scaffolded reading comprehension intervention involving small groups rotating through four metacognitive strategies: (1) **Summarizing** — capturing main points to check integration; (2) **Question generating** — formulating key questions to promote elaboration; (3) **Clarifying** — addressing confusing terms or passages; (4) **Predicting** — using text structure to anticipate upcoming content. Each strategy constitutes a component of the metacognitive monitoring-and-control cycle.
> **Source:** Section 7.2 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #reciprocal-teaching #reading-instruction #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Zone of Proximal Development, and how does it apply to reading comprehension instruction?
> **A:** **A:** Vygotsky's ZPD defines the range of tasks a learner cannot manage independently but can manage with appropriate support. In reading instruction, the ZPD identifies texts and tasks where comprehension is possible with scaffolding but would fail without it. Optimal instruction targets the ZPD — neither too easy (no challenge) nor too difficult (incomprehensible even with support). Scaffolding is gradually reduced as the learner's independent competence develops (I-We-You progression).
> **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #vygotsky #scaffolding #zone-of-proximal-development

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is conditional metacognitive knowledge, and why is it more powerful than declarative or procedural metacognitive knowledge for reading?
> **A:** **A:** Conditional metacognitive knowledge is knowing *when* and *why* to apply specific reading strategies — understanding the match between strategy type, text type, and comprehension goal. It is more powerful than declarative knowledge (knowing strategies exist) or procedural knowledge (knowing how to execute them) because it enables strategic flexibility: deploying the right strategy in the right context rather than applying favorite strategies indiscriminately. This strategy-selection capacity is the metacognitive master skill.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #conditional-knowledge #strategy-selection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is elaborative interrogation, and what mechanism explains its effectiveness?
> **A:** **A:** Elaborative interrogation is a reading strategy involving self-generated "Why is this true?" questions after encountering factual or explanatory claims. Its effectiveness is explained by multiple converging mechanisms: (1) prior knowledge activation (retrieving related schemas); (2) semantic elaboration (connecting the claim to a knowledge network, creating multiple retrieval pathways); (3) inference generation (producing causal inferences beyond text information); (4) comprehension monitoring (inability to generate a plausible "why" signals comprehension failure).
> **Source:** Section 5.2 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation #deep-processing

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control model, and what is the key distinction it draws?
> **A:** **A:** The Nelson-Narens (1990) model describes metacognitive functioning as a two-level system: the *object level* (the actual cognitive reading processes — word recognition, sentence parsing, situation model construction) and the *meta level* (the monitoring and control system operating on object-level processes). *Monitoring* = information flow from object to meta level (the meta system's observation of comprehension state). *Control* = information flow from meta to object level (executive regulation of reading behavior in response to monitoring). Key insight: monitoring and control are distinct — readers can monitor failures without deploying appropriate control responses.
> **Source:** Section 3.2 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #metacognition #nelson-narens #monitoring-control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Active Listening
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes *active* listening from *passive* listening, and what cognitive resources does it require?
> **A:** Active listening is a deliberate, metacognitively monitored practice of attending to a speaker's full communicative signal (verbal + nonverbal + paraverbal), checking one's own interpretive biases in real time, and demonstrating comprehension through reflective paraphrase. It requires sustained [[Attention]], [[Working-Memory]] allocation, [[Metacognitive-Regulation]], and [[Emotional-Regulation]] capacity — making it substantially more cognitively demanding than the passive receiving typically mistaken for listening.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Co-Construction vs. Transmission
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fundamental difference between the transmission model and co-constructive models of communication, and why does it matter practically?
> **A:** Transmission models (e.g., [[Shannon-Weaver-Information-Theory]]) treat meaning as residing in messages, transmitted from sender to receiver. Co-constructive models hold that meaning is jointly constructed by both parties, shaped by their individual [[Mental-Models]], [[Schema-Theory|schemas]], emotional states, and relational contexts. Practically: communication failures attributed to "unclear sending" often actually involve schema mismatch, relational framing misalignment, or cognitive bias — problems the transmission model cannot account for and therefore cannot address.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Process: Hattie-Timperley Feedback Levels
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four levels of feedback in the Hattie-Timperley model, and which are most developmentally effective?
> **A:** (1) Task level — correctness of specific work products; (2) Process level — strategies and processes underlying performance; (3) Self-regulation level — building capacity for self-monitoring and self-correction; (4) Self level — praise or criticism of the person themselves. Process and self-regulation level feedback are most developmentally effective; self-level feedback (particularly global praise or criticism) is largely ineffective for long-term development. See [[Hattie-&-Timperley-Feedback-Model]], [[Formative-Feedback]].

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
